</session_state>

Hi there! I'm curious about data visualisation and would love some clarity on the difference between a bar chart and a histogram. When should I use each type? Thanks!
Output
A great question — these two look similar but serve different purposes.

**Bar chart**
- Used for **categorical data** — comparing distinct groups or categories (e.g. sales by region, votes by candidate, revenue by product).
- The bars represent separate, unrelated categories, so there are gaps between them.
- Order of bars is often arbitrary or based on ranking/preference — you can rearrange them freely.

**Histogram**
- Used for **continuous numerical data** — showing the distribution of a single variable by grouping values into ranges ("bins") (e.g. distribution of ages, test scores, or heights).
- The bars are adjacent with no gaps, because they represent a continuous range being sliced into intervals — the x-axis is a number line, not discrete labels.
- Order is fixed (it follows the numeric scale), and bin width affects how the distribution looks.

**Quick rule of thumb:**
- Comparing named/categorical groups → **bar chart**
- Showing how a numeric variable is distributed across a range → **histogram**

If you want to try this out on actual data — say you have a spreadsheet and want to see whether a bar chart or histogram fits best, or want one built as an interactive chart — just share the file and I can bring in our Data Analyst to put it together for you!
Metrics (4)

The response directly and clearly answers the question by explaining the difference between bar charts and histograms, including when to use each, fulfilling the helpfulness criterion. The tone is professional yet friendly and conversational, suitable for a mid-level programme manager, as seen in phrases like 'A great question' and the offer to assist with data files. The explanation is concise and well-structured with bullet points and a quick rule of thumb, avoiding verbosity while providing sufficient detail. The only minor shortcoming is a slight extension in the closing offer, which, while friendly, could be seen as slightly beyond the core question.
